Deadlock-free Context-free Session Types Conference: The 27th International Symposium on Principles and Practice of Declarative Programming; September 10–11; Rende, Italy
Andreia Mordido, Jorge A. Pérez

Abstract
. We consider the problem of statically ensuring that message-passing programs never run into deadlocks . We focus on concurrent functional programs governed by context-free session types , which can express rich tree-like structures not expressible in standard session types. Existing techniques based on context-free session types enforce protocol conformance but not deadlock freedom. We propose a type system that enhances context-free session types with a priority-based approach to deadlock freedom, considering polymorphic and recursive types. Interestingly, the notions needed for avoiding deadlocks fit nicely into this expressive setting. We prove that well-typed programs respect their protocols and never run into deadlocks at run-time; we also demonstrate the expressiveness gains with respect to prior work by means of examples.



1.
Introduction

Writing correct concurrent programs is notoriously hard.
A long-standing issue is ensuring the absence of
deadlocks
: situations in which concurrent processes are forever blocked awaiting a message.
In today’s distributed infrastructures, even a single component that becomes blocked can severely impact system availability.
Motivated by this challenge, in this work we develop new compositional verification techniques that ensure deadlock freedom for processes that exchange messages by following some predetermined
protocols
.
We focus on static verification based on
session types
, which specify reciprocal protocols on heterogeneously typed
channels
(
Honda 1993
;
Honda et al. 1998
)
.
As key novelty, we consider
context-free
session types
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
)
, in which advanced forms of sequential composition and non-regular recursion enable us to specify tree-like structures not expressible in standard session types. By targeting this expressive class of session types, our work immediately and significantly broadens the range of concurrent processes that can be statically verified to be protocol compliant and deadlock-free.

The substantial expressiveness of context-free session types is known to lead to challenges in the study of central notions, such as type equivalence
(
Almeida et al. 2020a
)
and subtyping
(
Silva et al. 2023
)
. Addressing deadlock freedom is not an exception; there are two noteworthy points.

First
, statically enforcing deadlock freedom entails performing
non-local analyses
on the behavior of interacting processes. The goal is identifying circular dependencies between processes that cannot be detected by analyzing each process in isolation. Existing disciplines based on context-free session types prioritize programming flexibility over stringent verification; they rely on purely local analyses that may accept deadlocked processes as typable.

Second
, there is no “obviously small” formulation of context-free session types for studying deadlock freedom. While for standard session types representative deadlock analyses can be done in a setting without recursion, in the case of context-free session types one must necessarily include both recursion and polymorphism. Indeed, in context-free session
types recursion and polymorphism are “entangled”
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
)
: limiting recursion would make
our system degenerate into standard session types;
removing polymorphism would prevent us from having recursion.
Defined on top of a functional language with concurrency, our
context-free session types, therefore, combine three distinctive features:
sequential composition, non-regular (equi)recursion, and polymorphism.

We handle this formulation of context-free session types rather smoothly, as a simple extension of the original proposal by Thiemann and Vasconcelos
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
)
. To carry out the non-local analyses required, we follow the
priority-based

approach pioneered by Kobayashi
(
Kobayashi 2006
;
Kobayashi 2002
)
and later streamlined by Padovani
(
Padovani 2014a
)
. The key idea is to endow each communication action in processes/terms with a priority, a number that denotes its urgency: immediately enabled actions have a higher priority than actions not currently enabled (i.e., which appear later on in the process). This idea allows to detect processes that are stuck due to insidious circular dependencies between threads, as their associated actions cannot be given a valid priority. The approach extends to processes with recursion, in which the management of priorities requires care.

Originally developed for process calculi (variants of the
\pi
-calculus) with
linear types

(
Kobayashi et al. 1999
)
, the priority-based approach supports the analysis of processes organized in circular network topologies; here we follow the type systems by Padovani and Novara
(
Padovani and Novara 2015
;
Padovani and Novara 2014
)
, who adopted the priority-based approach in
(
Padovani 2014a
)
to higher-order functional programs with concurrency.
Following their approach, but casting it in the more expressive setting of context-free session types, we develop a type system and associated meta-theoretical results of
type preservation
and
deadlock-freedom
.

Contributions

This paper makes the following contributions:

•

We endow context-free session types with a priority-based mechanism to statically guarantee deadlock freedom.

•

We upgrade priority abstractions to polymorphic types, enabling polymorphic recursion at the level of priorities.

•

We present several examples demonstrating the gain in expressiveness compared with prior works.

Outline

Section

2
motivates our work via examples; we illustrate the gain in expressivity with respect to other works in
Sections

2.3
and
2.4
.

Section

3
introduces the type language and notions of type duality and priorities.

Section

4
introduces expressions, configurations, and the typing rules.

Section

5
presents the operational semantics and proves type preservation and deadlock-freedom.

Section

6
discusses related work and

Section

7
concludes the paper.

2.
Deadlocks in Message-Passing Programs

This section provides a brief overview of our developments.
We consider several examples written in FreeST
(
Almeida et al. 2019
)
, a functional programming language with context-free
session types.

2.1.
A Classic Deadlock Scenario

The classic example of a deadlock in message-passing
programs involves two processes that share two channels. They
attempt to send in one channel the value received in the other, but do so in an order that prevents synchronization.
Each process holds two linear endpoints, one per channel.
The two endpoints of the same channel must have
dual
behavior: if one endpoint sends, the other receives (and vice versa).
In terms of session types, one endpoint should be governed by the type

T

=

?
Int
;

Wait
, which says that the endpoint
first
receives
an integer and then
waits
to be closed.
This type showcases the sequential composition operator ‘
;
’.
The other endpoint must then be governed by the type dual to
T
, which first
sends
an integer and then
closes
the channel (denoted
!
Int
;

Close
).

In FreeST
(
Almeida et al. 2019
)
, each process can be implemented as follows:

⬇

1

type

T

=

?
Int
;

Wait

2

3

f

:

T

\rightarrow

dualof

T

1
\rightarrow

()

4

f

x

y

=

5

let

(
n
,
x
)

=

receive

x

in

6

let

y

=

send

n

y

in

7

wait

x
;

8

close

y

The two arguments of
f
(
x
and
y
)
represent endpoints of two distinct channels.
Their types are, respectively,
T
and
dualof

T
, which implicitly denotes
!
Int
;

Close
.
Notice that the second arrow represents a
linear
function (denoted by
1
\rightarrow
) which must be used
once
, since its context contains a linear channel (of type
T
).

In functional implementations of sessions, such as FreeST, functions operate on endpoints and consume them gradually according to their protocol.
This way, given an endpoint
x
, the function
receive

x
returns a pair: the received value and an endpoint (the continuation of
x
); similarly, given a value
v
and an endpoint
y
, the function
send

v

y
returns the continuation of
y
.
Also,
wait

x
and
close

y
are explicit mechanisms for closing a channel. With these intuitions in place, we see that
f

proceeds as prescribed by its type: it receives on

x
, sends on
y
, and then closes both channels.

Consider now the following function
main
, which first uses the
new
operation to create fresh identifiers for the endpoints, and then launches two processes in parallel, each of them running an instance of function
f
(with appropriate parameters in each case):

⬇

9

main

:

()

10

main

=

11

let

(
r1
,

w1
)

=

new

@
T

()

in

12

let

(
r2
,

w2
)

=

new

@
T

()

in

13

fork

(\
_
:()

1
\rightarrow

f

r1

w2
);

14

f

r2

w1

Note that this program type-checks: the
behavior of the channels (receiving/sending an integer and closing)
is locally respected.
Now, it is not difficult to see that the resulting program enters into a
deadlock
:
both processes are blocked, waiting to
receive on both channels, and neither is enabled to send the value to be
received.

Addressing deadlocks using priorities.

To avoid getting blocked at run-time, one solution is to enrich the
types so as to enable a more informed
static analysis
of
the order of operations in the program body. Following
Padovani and Novara
(
Padovani and Novara 2014
;
Padovani and Novara 2015
)
, we annotate the types with the
priorities
associated to the corresponding
operation.
(Throughout the document we highlight the new additions to
the type system—the priorities—with colour
teal
.)

Revisiting our classic deadlock scenario,
the types that govern the two channels should now be typed
differently, priority-wise:

⬇

1

type

R

=

?
p
Int
;

Wait

(p+2)

2

type

S

=

?
q
Int
;

Wait

(q+2)

Type
R
extends
T
with priorities: it receives an
Int
at priority
p

and then waits at priority
p+2
, while
type
S
is similar: it extends
T
under priority

q
.
To showcase the use of priorities, consider a variant of
main
that launches the two functions
f
and
g
:

⬇

3

f

:

R

\rightarrow
[top,

bot]

dualof

S

1
\rightarrow
[p,

q+2]

()

4

f

x

y

=

5

let

(
n
,
x
)

=

receive

x

in

–

priority:

p

6

let

y

=

send

n

y

in

–

priority:

q

7

wait

x
;

–

priority:

p+2

8

close

y

–

priority:

q+2

9

10

g

:

S

\rightarrow
[top,

bot]

dualof

R

1
\rightarrow
[q,

p+2]

()

11

g

y

x

=

12

let

(
n
,
y
)

=

receive

y

in

–

priority:

q

13

let

x

=

send

n

x

in

–

priority:

p

14

wait

y
;

–

priority:

q+2

15

close

x

–

priority:

p+2

Following Padovani and Novara
(
Padovani and Novara 2014
;
Padovani and Novara 2015
)
,
we use priority annotations on arrow types: they provide
boundaries
for the input/output behavior of a function.
The annotations in the first arrow of
f
mean that
there is no
channel in the function environment (represented by

top
) and that there is no
latent effect
after
function application (represented by
bot
); for
the second arrow,
x
is already in the
environment (represented with priority
p
in the
annotation) and after application we expect
to have operations up to priority
q+2
.
The annotations for the arrows in the type of
g
are analogous.

We can now follow each function by considering the involved priorities; we detail them on the right of each instruction.
We realize that we have a mismatch:
according to the order of operations (and the typing rules
presented in
Section

4
), function
f

requires
p
<
q
(Lines 3 and 4)
whereas function
g
requires
q
<
p
(Lines 10 and 11).
This mismatch is detected during type checking, when the (updated)
main
function launches
f
and
g
in parallel threads.
There are no
p
and
q
that satisfy both requirements, which is sufficient grounds to statically identify a deadlock.
The programmer must then refactor their program
to ensure that the order of operations meets the corresponding
priorities. In this case, it suffices to swap the operations
receive/send and wait/close in one of the functions.

2.2.
Deadlocks and Simple Recursion

Session types naturally take advantage of recursion and are
usually equi-recursive. As Padovani and Novara
observe
(
Padovani and Novara 2014
)
, recursion introduces its
challenges, which are actually very familiar to issues found in the theory of context-free
session types
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
)
.

Consider a (session) type
Stream
that sends an
infinite sequence of unit values. This may be specified as

\mu{\beta}.{{!\mathsf{Unit}};{\beta}}
, where
\beta
denotes a recursive variable. We could write a program for two communicating
Stream
s as
follows (noting that
()
represents the
\mathsf{Unit}

type in FreeST):

⬇

1

type

Stream1

=

!
p
()

;

Stream1

2

type

Stream2

=

!
q
()

;

Stream2

3

4

client

:

Stream1

\rightarrow
[top,bot]

dualof

Stream2

5

1
\rightarrow
[p,q]

()

6

client

c1

c2

=

7

let

c1

=

send

()

c1

in

–

priority:

p

8

let

(
_
,
c2
)

=

receive

c2

in

–

priority:

q

9

client

c1

c2

With an “appropriate” communicating
server
,
the program above could successfully pass the type check.
However, due to the equirecursive nature of our types,
nothing prevents us from anticipating that the client will
do more than one pair send/receive message and trying to write
the following (behaviourally equivalent) program:

⬇

10

client
’

:

Stream1

\rightarrow
[top,bot]

dualof

Stream2

11

1
\rightarrow
[p,q]

()

12

client
’

c1

c2

=

13

–

first

send/receive

14

let

c1

=

send

()

c1

in

–priority:

p

15

let

(
_
,
c2
)

=

receive

c2

in

–priority:

q

16

–

second

send/receive

17

let

c1

=

send

()

c1

in

–priority:

p

(!)

18

let

(
_
,
c2
)

=

receive

c2

in

–priority:

q

(!)

19

client
’

c1

c2

In Lines 7 and 8, we see that equi-recursion re-introduces
priorities that make the program ill-typed. Revisiting the
thorough analysis by Thiemann et
al.
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
;
Almeida et al. 2022
)
, we realize that
the need to enable
polymorphic recursion
is now
apparent at the level of priorities. To that end, we
propose
priority abstraction
to allow the type to evolve
without entering an inconsistent state.
Along the lines of type abstraction, we use a quantifier

to abstract over priorities
and bind a
priority
variable

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}
to a
priority interval

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}
, as
proposed by Padovani
and Novara
(
Padovani and Novara 2014
)
.
Using priority abstraction, the type
Stream
can then be defined by:

\mu{\beta}.{({\mathchoice{\hbox to8.31pt{\vbox to7.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.69435pt\lower-3.9055pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}{{}}{

A channel of this type starts by instantiating the

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}

. Then, it sends a unit value and finally recurs and
starts all over again.

2.3.
Deadlocks and Non-Regular Recursion

Context-free session types shine with
non-regular recursion
.
The classic example that capitalises on all the features of
context-free session types is exchanging binary
trees
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
;
Almeida et al. 2020a
)
.
Consider the type

A channel governed by this type starts by
instantiating the priority variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}

and then offers two branches: one with label

\mathsf{LeafC}
that proceeds by doing nothing (represented
by type
\mathsf{Skip}
), and another
with label
\mathsf{NodeC}
, that receives an integer
under priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}+1}
and then receives the
left subtree followed by the right subtree.

Consider the implementation of function
receiveTree
under
our priority-based approach to deadlock-freedom:

⬇

1

data

Tree

=

Leaf

|

Node

Int

Tree

Tree

2

3

type

TreeChannel

=

\forall
p

p

\in

(bot,top)

\Rightarrow

4

&
p
{
LeafC
:

Skip
,

5

NodeC
:

?
(p+1)
Int
;
TreeChannel
;
TreeChannel
}

6

7

receiveTree

:

\forall
p

p

\in

(bot,top)

\Rightarrow

8

TreeChannel
;

a

\rightarrow
[top,p+1]

(
Tree
,

a
)

9

receiveTree

c

=

10

match

c
{p}

with

{

11

LeafC

c

\rightarrow

12

(
Leaf
,

c
),

13

NodeC

c

\rightarrow

14

let

(
x
,

c
)

=

receive

c

in

15

let

(
left
,

c
)

=

receiveTree
{p+2}

@
(
TreeChannel

;

a
)

c

in

16

let

(
right
,

c
)

=

receiveTree
{p+4}

@
a

c

in

17

(
Node

x

left

right
,

c
)

18

}

The signature of
receiveTree
states
that the function holds under some priority
p
and
receives a channel of type
TreeChannel
;

a
, where

a
is a
type variable
. (FreeST implicitly binds
all type variables occurring free in a function signature.)
This type variable is instantiated in a function call
through the
@
operator (Lines 15 and 16).
Finally, it should return a pair with a reconstruction of the

Tree
and the continuation of the input channel.
The function
match
handles the
choices on
c
, instantiated with priority

p
: for
LeafC
it
returns a pair with a
Leaf
and the
continuation of the channel; for
NodeC
, the
function receives the integer on channel

c
and then receives the left and right subtrees.

Notice that the recursive calls apply polymorphic recursion
on both types and priorities: in the first call (Line 15),
the priority variable needs to be
instantiated with the updated priority
p+2
and
the type variable is updated with the
type of the continuation of the channel
(
TreeChannel
;
a
). In the second call the
priorities need to account for the latent effect in the
function application in line 15 and the reminder of the
channel has now type
a
. Finally, the function
returns a pair composed of the reconstruction of the node
followed by the continuation of the channel.

Lines 15 and 16 illustrate the harmonious way in which our
context-free session types integrate priorities: the
programmers are already used to reason about the
instantiation channels at each recursive
call
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
;
Almeida et al. 2020a
)
.
With negligible overhead they do this reasoning
at the level of priorities.

2.4.
Polymorphic Recursion over Priorities

By abstracting over priorities and handling priorities and
types seamlessly, we gain expressive power when compared
to Padovani and Novara’s
displacements

(
Padovani and Novara 2014
)
, which shift
priorities in recursive calls. Displacements have
some limitations, as acknowledged by the
authors. Indeed, they identify
a
non-regular
pattern of the
filterStream

function, which forwards values received depending on whether
the filter validates the values or not. This introduces
an asymmetry on the number of unfolds of the

sender stream
when compared to the

receiver stream
. As we are able to tune the
instantiation of priorities in each function call, we can
now correctly implement and validate such function:

⬇

1

type

InputStream

=

\forall
p

p

\in

(bot,top)

\Rightarrow

2

?
p

Int
;

InputStream

3

4

filterStream

:

\forall
p

p

\in

(bot,top),

q

\in

(p,top)

\Rightarrow

5

(
Int

\rightarrow
[top,bot]

Bool
)

6

\rightarrow
[top,bot]

InputStream

7

\rightarrow
[top,bot]

dualof

InputStream

8

1
\rightarrow
[p,q+1]

()

9

filterStream

f

x

y

=

10

let

(
v
,

x
)

=

receive

x
{p}

in

–

priority

:

p

11

if

(
f

v
)

then

12

let

y

=

send

v

y
{q}

in

–

priority

:

q

13

filterStream
{p+1}{q+1}

f

x

y

14

else

15

filterStream
{p+1}{q+1}

f

x

y

Our approach is able to correctly type cases that do not
folow a “regular
pattern”. Indeed, context-free session types traditionally
handle
non-regular
patterns, after all.

Having illustrated our type systems via examples, we now formally introduce it and establish its properties.

3.
Context-Free Session Types, with Priorities

Types are split into two different syntactic categories:
functional types and session types.
The syntax of types relies on some base sets:

\ell\in L
stands for labels,
\alpha
stands for
functional type variables,
\beta
stands for session type
variables, and
m
stands for multiplicities (relevant for arrow types).
As we have seen,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}}
stand for priorities, priority variables, and priority intervals, respectively.
We use
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}
to stand for the lowest and highest
priority, respectively.
We use
\gamma
as a meta-variable for type variables (functional or session).
The full grammar of types is in
Fig.

1
.

(Multiplicities)

\displaystyle m\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{lin}

linear

\displaystyle\mathsf{un}

unrestricted

(Functional types)

\displaystyle T,U\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{Unit}

unit type

\displaystyle\;\mid\;

\displaystyle{T}\rightarrow_{m}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{2}}}{U}

function type

\displaystyle\;\mid\;

\displaystyle{T}\times{U}

product type

\displaystyle\;\mid\;

\displaystyle\alpha

(functional) type variable

\displaystyle\;\mid\;

\displaystyle\mu{\alpha}.{T}

recursive functional type

\displaystyle\;\mid\;

\displaystyle\forall{\alpha}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}.{T}

polymorphic functional type

\displaystyle\;\mid\;

\displaystyle\forall{\beta}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}.{S}

polymorphic session type

priority abstraction

Figure 1.
Syntax of types.

(Priorities)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\pi}\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}n}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\sqcap{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\sqcup{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}\;\mid\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}

(Intervals)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}]\;\mid\;({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}]\;\mid\;[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}})\;\mid\;({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}})

Figure 2.
Syntax of priorities and priority variables.

(Polymorphic variables)

\displaystyle\Delta\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle\epsilon\;\mid\;\Delta,\alpha::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}\;\mid\;\Delta,\beta::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}\qquad

(Priority variables)

\displaystyle\Theta\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle\epsilon\;\mid\;\Theta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}}\qquad

Figure 3.
Polymorphic and priority typing contexts.

Notice that by considering two syntactic categories
we avoid the need for a kinding system
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
;
Almeida et al. 2022
)
, but
entails some duplication: polymorphic types can
either have a
functional
body, in which case the
quantifier binds a

functional
type variable, or the body can be a session,
in which case the quantifier binds a
session
type
variable; the same for recursive types. This means that in addition
to
\alpha\neq\beta
, we also have two different (but overloaded)

\forall
and
\mu
operators.
Recursive
types keep their body’s original “nature”, whereas polymorphic
types are always functional. (We leave the details of this
choice to previous work on context-free session
types
(
Almeida et al. 2020a
)
.)

As discussed in
Section

2
, the distinguishing feature of our proposal is the
incorporation of priority
annotations
(
Padovani and Novara 2014
;
Padovani and Novara 2015
;
Kokke and Dardha 2023
)
in
context-free session
types
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
)
.
The function type

{T}\rightarrow_{m}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{2}}}{U}
is decorated with a

lower priority bound

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}
indicating that the closure of
the function contains channels with priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}
or
greater, and an
upper priority bound

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{2}}
standing for
the maximum priority that is pending in the function and
will be triggered once the function is applied (see the comprehensive
explanation by
Padovani and Novara
(
Padovani and Novara 2014
)
for further details).
The multiplicity
m
determines how many times the function can be used:

\mathsf{lin}
means that it can be used once,

\mathsf{un}
means that the function can be used zero or
more times.

In polymorphic types (
\forall{\alpha}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}.{T},\forall{\beta}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}.{S}
), the type variable is assigned to a
priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}
. Priorities are
defined in terms of priority variables
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}
, that are
abstracted through a
\textstyle\forall
binder that assigns to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}
a
priority interval
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}}
. Following prior works
(
Padovani 2014b
;
Kokke and Dardha 2023
;
Padovani and Novara 2014
)
,
the exchange of messages (send/receive, internal/external choice) and close/wait operate under a priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}
. Type
\mathsf{Skip}
and sequential composition
do not explicitly represent an operation, so do not require any priority annotation.
We denote the set of
free
type variables
in a type
T
as
\mathsf{ftv}({T})
and the set of
free priority variables
as
\mathsf{fpv}({T})
.

Fig.

2
gives the syntax of priorities and
priority intervals, which follows Padovani and Novara’s work
(
Padovani and Novara 2014
)
. Types with priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}
correspond to
so-called
unrestricted types

(
Vasconcelos 2012
)
.
Priorities can also be integer values
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}n}
,
priority variables
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}
, the maximum or minimum
of two priorities (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\sqcup{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\sqcap{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}
, respectively) or
a
displacement

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}
,
representing the addition of integers extended with the
following rules
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}
. Priority intervals can
either be closed or open to account for the inclusion of
the limits.

Type formation

Not all types are well formed. For example, we do not want
type
!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}2}}{(!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}1}}{\mathsf{Int}})}
to be well-formed since
the operation that follows in the payload is
more urgent
,
i.e., has lower priority, than sending the message itself.
We also do not wish to define the type

\forall{\alpha}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}}.{?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}{\alpha}}
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}

are not properly defined.

Paving our way to type formation, typing contexts for
polymorphic and priority variables are
presented in
Fig.

3
. We record
polymorphic variables
with the corresponding priority assignment in environment

\Delta
and priority variables with the respective
range in environment
\Theta
.

To properly control the order of operations, we often need to refer to the priority of a type.
Given a type
T
, its priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|T|_{\Theta}}
is defined as the lowest priority of its
operations—see
Fig.

4
.
Note in particular that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\mathsf{Skip}|_{\Theta}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}
and that the priority of

{S_{1}};{S_{2}}
follows that of
{S_{1}}
.
Our comparison relations should be considered
up to the assignments in
\Theta

(
Padovani and Novara 2014
)
,
which will be denoted with a subscript
\Theta
.
For instance: for
\Theta_{0}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{1}}\in({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{2}}\in[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{1}}),{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{3}}\in({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}}]\}
, the relations

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{2}}<_{\Theta_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{1}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{1}}<_{\Theta_{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{3}}
hold.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\mathsf{Skip}|_{\Theta}}=\,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\mathsf{Unit}|_{\Theta}}=\,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}{T}|_{\Theta}}=\,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|{T}\rightarrow_{m}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{2}}}{U}|_{\Theta}}=\,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}{T}|_{\Theta}}=\,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|{T}\times{U}|_{\Theta}}=\,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|T|_{\Theta}}}\sqcap{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|U|_{\Theta}}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\&^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}\{{\ell}\colon{S_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in L}|_{\Theta}}=\,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\alpha|_{\Theta}}=\,

\displaystyle\Theta(\alpha)\text{ if $\alpha\in\mathsf{dom}({\Theta})$}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\oplus^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}\{{\ell}\colon{S_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in L}|_{\Theta}}=\,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\forall{\alpha}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}.{T}|_{\Theta}}=\,

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|{S_{1}};{S_{2}}|_{\Theta}}=\,

Priority of types.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}}}.{T}}}\quad\textsc{{F-PAbs}}\quad\frac{{\Delta,\gamma::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}\quad\text{T contractive}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\mu{\gamma}.{T}}}\quad\textsc{{F-Rec}}\quad\frac{\gamma\in\mathsf{dom}({\Delta})}{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\gamma}}\quad\textsc{{F-Var}}

Figure 5.
Type formation.

The judgement
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}

denotes that type
T
is well-formed under the polymorphic context
\Delta

and the priority context
\Theta
. The rules for type formation are
defined in
Fig.

5
. The type formation rules

F-Unit
,
F-Abs
,
F-Pair
,
F-Close
,
F-Wait
,

F-Skip
and
F-Var
are standard.
Rule
F-MsgOut
(resp.,
F-MsgIn
) requires that the priority
of the send (resp., receive) operation is lower than the priority of the payload.
Rules
F-IntChoice
and
F-ExtChoice
require the
choice operation to be
more urgent
than the continuation types.

Rule
F-TAbs
states that a polymorphic type
\forall{\gamma}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}.{T}

can only assign a type variable
\gamma

to a priority variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}
if all the free priority variables occurring
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}
are defined in
\Theta
; furthermore,
T
should be well-typed
under the extended environment
\Delta,\gamma::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}
.
Similarly, rule
F-PAbs
states that a priority variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}

can only be assigned to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}
through the
\textstyle\forall
binder
if all the free priority variables occurring in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}
are defined
in
\Theta
; furthermore, the body of the type should be well-typed
under the priority environment
\Theta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}
.

Rule
F-Rec
states that a recursive type
\mu{\gamma}.{T}
is
well-typed if
T
is well-typed under the extended context
\Delta,\gamma::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}

and
T
is contractive, meaning that
T
should have no subterms
of the form
\mu\alpha.\mu\alpha_{1}.\ldots\mu\alpha_{n}.\alpha
, considering
an adaption of the conventional notion of
contractivity
(
Gay and Hole 2005
)
to account
for
\mathsf{Skip}
as the neutral element of the sequential composition. (A rule-based
definition of contractivity can be found in previous
works
(
Almeida et al. 2020a
;
Silva et al. 2023
)
.)

Finally, Rule
F-Seq
states that a sequential composition
{T_{1}};{T_{2}}

is well-formed if both
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are. It may seem
natural to impose the restriction

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|T_{1}|_{\Theta}}<_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|T_{2}|_{\Theta}}
, but this would
invalidate the monoidal properties of context-free session types, namely that

\mathsf{Skip}
is the neutral element of sequential composition and can, therefore,
be used without jeopardising the well-formedness of types. For example, the
type
{!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}1}}{\mathsf{Int}}};{{\mathsf{Skip}};{?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}2}}{\mathsf{Unit}}}}
can be used
where the type
{!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}1}}{\mathsf{Int}}};{?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}2}}{\mathsf{Unit}}}
is expected (see the typing rule
T-Eq
). Note that, by its nature,

\mathsf{Skip}
is an
invisible type
, i.e., it does not exhibit behaviour and
is uninhabited. This means that we will be able to impose this natural

priority ordering
at
the level of the typing rules (
Section

4
).

Type duality

Duality is paramount to establish
compatible
communication
between two endpoints of a channel. This notion is defined only
over session types and is presented in
Fig.

6
.
The definition is
standard
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
;
Almeida et al. 2020a
)
. Since we are dealing with higher-order sessions,
in the sense that channels can be conveyed into messages,
the duality of recursive types relies on

negative references
, which serve to dualize
recursive sessions properly
(
Almeida et al. 2025
)
.
Duality preserves priorities:
for that reason, the dual of a priority abstraction
(

Type duality on session types.

4.
Statics: Configurations and Typing Rules

\displaystyle c\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{fork}\;\mid\;\mathsf{send}\;\mid\;\mathsf{receive}\;\mid\;\mathsf{close}\;\mid\;\mathsf{wait}\;\mid\;\,()\,\;\mid\;\mathsf{select}\,{k}\;\mid\;\mathsf{fix}

(Constants)

\displaystyle v\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle x\;\mid\;c\;\mid\;\lambda_{m}{x}:{T}.{e}\;\mid\;({v},{v})\;\mid\;\Lambda{\alpha}.{v}\;\mid\;\mathbb{\Lambda}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}.{v}\;\mid\;{\mathsf{close}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}\;\mid\;{\mathsf{wait}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}\;\mid\;{\mathsf{fork}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}\;\mid\;{{\mathsf{fork}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}\;\mid\;{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}

\displaystyle\;\mid\;{{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]\;\mid\;{{{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}\}\;\mid\;{{{{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}\}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\pi}}\}\;\mid\;{{{{{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}\}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\pi}}\}}[{S}]\;\mid\;{{{{{{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}\}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\pi}}\}}[{S}]}{v}

\displaystyle\;\mid\;{\mathsf{receive}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}\;\mid\;{{\mathsf{receive}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]\;\mid\;{{{\mathsf{receive}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}\;\mid\;{{{{\mathsf{receive}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\pi}}}\}\;\mid\;{{{{{\mathsf{receive}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\pi}}}\}}[{S}]

(Values)

\displaystyle e\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle v\;\mid\;{e}{e}\;\mid\;({e},{e})\;\mid\;\mathsf{let}\,({x_{1}},{x_{2}})={e}\,\mathsf{in}\,{e}\;\mid\;{e};{e}\;\mid\;\mathsf{new}\,{T}\;\mid\;\mathsf{match}\,{e}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{{\ell}\rightarrow{e_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in L}\;\mid\;{e}[{T}]\;\mid\;{e}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}

(Expressions)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle{\phi}\langle{e}\rangle\;\mid\;{\mathcal{C}}\,\|\,{\mathcal{C}}\;\mid\;(\nu{x}{y})\,{\mathcal{C}}

(Configurations)

\displaystyle\phi\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle\circ\;\mid\;\bullet

(Thread flags)

\displaystyle\Gamma\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle\epsilon\;\mid\;\Gamma,x:T

(Typing contexts)

Figure 7.
The syntax of values, expressions, configurations and typing contexts.

Our term language is a call-by-value concurrent language that extends earlier proposals for context-free
session types
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
;
Almeida et al. 2020a
)
,
incorporating applications and abstractions over priority
variables.

Fig.

7
presents the syntax of constants, values, expressions and configurations.
As customary in functional languages with sessions (cf.
(
Gay and Vasconcelos 2010
)
), session communication is defined at the level of
configurations
, which is where deadlocks could occur.
Formally, a configuration is either an expression,
a parallel composition of configurations, or a configuration in the scope of a channel restriction
(\nu{x}{y})\,{-}
that binds together the endpoints
x
and
y
.
Differently from prior works on context-free session types, here we consider

flagged
configurations, which indicate whether the configuration is the main thread (
\phi=\bullet
) or is a child thread (
\phi=\circ
).

Further novelties concern priorities and their associated constructs.
Priority abstractions are of the form
\mathbb{\Lambda}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}.{e}
, where

\mathbb{\Lambda}
bounds a priority variable
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}
that might occur free
in
e
. Like type abstractions, priority abstractions of the form

\mathbb{\Lambda}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}.{v}
are values. Priority applications over
an expression
e
are denoted by
{e}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}
.
There are several forms of partial
applications for constants, which are considered values; they will be justified by
the types presented in
Fig.

10
.
We denote by
\mathsf{fv}({e})
the set of free (term) variables occurring in
e
.

Context formation.

Fig.

7
also defines the syntax of typing contexts for term variables.

Fig.

8
presents context formation, defined as usual from type formation.

Fig.

9
presents context split, which handles the distribution
of bindings depending on their priority, recalling that types with priority

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}
are unrestricted.

\displaystyle\frac{}{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\epsilon}}\quad\textsc{{Ctx-Emp}}\qquad\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}\qquad{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma,x:T}}\quad\textsc{{Ctx-NEmp}}\qquad

Figure 8.
Context formation rules.

\displaystyle\frac{}{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\epsilon}={\epsilon}\circ{\epsilon}}\quad\textsc{{S-Emp}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}={\Gamma_{1}}\circ{\Gamma_{2}}\qquad{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|T|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma,x:T}={(\Gamma_{1},x:T)}\circ{(\Gamma_{2},x:T)}}\quad\textsc{{S-Top}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}={\Gamma_{1}}\circ{\Gamma_{2}}\qquad{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|T|_{\Theta}}<_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma,x:T}={(\Gamma_{1},x:T)}\circ{\Gamma_{2}}}\quad\textsc{{S-Left}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}={\Gamma_{1}}\circ{\Gamma_{2}}\qquad{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|T|_{\Theta}}<_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma,x:T}={\Gamma_{1}}\circ{(\Gamma_{2},x:T)}}\quad\textsc{{S-Right}}

Figure 9.
Context split.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}{}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{3}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}}]}}.{\forall{\beta}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{3}}}.{{{?^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{2}}}{\alpha}};{\beta}}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{un}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{2}}}{{\alpha}\times{\beta}}}}}}}

\displaystyle\,()\,

Figure 10.
Types for constants.

Fig.

10
defines types for constants. By upgrading
priorities to the level of types, we can give proper types to all constants
instead of relying on type schemes, as in
(
Padovani and Novara 2014
;
Kokke and Dardha 2023
)
.
Following Padovani and Novara’s proposal
(
Padovani and Novara 2014
)
,
our priority binders control the order of operations
in a type. We discuss these types:

•

The constant
\mathsf{fork}
receives a thunk with whatever
priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{1}}
and latent effect
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{2}}
, returns

\,()\,
and has no latent effect (signalled with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}
as the second
annotation of the arrow).

•

The type of
\mathsf{send}
starts by binding the priority

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{1}}
of the payload, then requires the priority of the
send operation to be lower (i.e., more urgent) than that of the payload
and, finally, the lowest priority of the continuation channel should be
higher than that of the send operation. After all type and priority variables are instantiated, function
\mathsf{send}
expects an
input value to be sent, then expects an input channel of type

{!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{2}}}{\alpha}};{\beta}
and returns the continuation channel

\beta
. It has latent effect
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{2}}
, meaning that as soon
as function
\mathsf{send}
is fully applied, it triggers operations with
priority at most
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}_{2}}
.

•

The interpretation of the types for
\mathsf{receive}
,

\mathsf{close}
and
\mathsf{wait}
is similar to that of
\mathsf{send}
.

•

The type of
\mathsf{fix}
is standard; the
recursive functions presented in the examples use the
\mathsf{fix}
point
operator implicitly.

Having seen the types for constants, the different value shapes in
Fig.

7

become clearer. If we let ourselves be guided by the type of
\mathsf{send}
, we
realize that we can apply it to a priority and get a partial application

{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}
, or further make a type application and get

{{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]
. Then, we can choose to do one or two
priority applications again, reaching
{{{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}

and
{{{{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\pi}}\}
. At
this point, we are left with another type application yielding the value

{{{{{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\pi}}\}}[{S}]

which has type
{{T}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{un}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}}}{{!^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}{T}};{S}}}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{lin}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}{S}
.
Before we get
\mathsf{send}
fully applied, we can do a term-level application of the
previous expression to a value
v
and get the expression

{{{{{{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\pi}}\}}[{S}]}{v}
,
which is still considered a value.
For the other constants, the reasoning is similar.

The typing judgement
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\rho}}
means
that an expression
e
has type
T
and an upper bound
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}

on its priority under the typing environments
\Delta,\Gamma

and
\Theta
.

Fig.

11
presents our typing rules, which
result from incorporating the priority-based approach to
deadlock-freedom
(
Padovani and Novara 2014
;
Padovani and Novara 2015
;
Kokke and Dardha 2023
)

in the type system of context-free session types
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
;
Almeida et al. 2020a
)
.

We comment on the typing rules.
Constants are
typed through
T-Const
according to the types
assigned in
Fig.

10
; they have no
effect (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}
) and assume that the typing context

\Gamma
has priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}
, i.e., it is

unrestricted
. Rule
T-Var
also requires
that variables are typed under an unrestricted environment,
with no effect.

We have three forms of abstraction (on types, priorities, and terms).
Rule
T-TAbs
states that a type
abstraction
\Lambda{\alpha}.{e}
has type
\forall{\alpha}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}.{T}
with no effect if
e
has type
T
under the extended
polymorphic environment
\Delta,\alpha::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}
, assuming
that the free priority variables occurring in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}
are
defined in
\Theta
and that the typing environment
\Gamma
is
unrestricted—because we do not want to abstract over linear values.
Rule
T-PAbs
is similar with the expected adaption to priority abstractions.
Rules
T-AbsLin
and
T-AbsUn
consider linear and unrestricted functions, respectively; in the latter case, the context
\Gamma
should be unrestricted.
In terms of priorities, both rules
state that the
lower priority bound coincides with the lowest
priority in the typing context
\Gamma
and the upper
priority bound is the effect of evaluating the body of
the function.

Rule
T-TApp
is standard with the
extra proviso that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|T|_{\Theta}}
coincides with

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}
under the priority context
\Theta
.
Rule
T-PApp
is new
and states that a priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}
can instantiate

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}
belongs to the priority interval assigned to

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}
; all the occurrences of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}
in
U

are updated with the new value
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}
. Rule
T-App
is standard
for
call-by-value
evaluation strategies
(
Padovani and Novara 2014
)
: it requires the first expression to have a

more urgent
priority than the typing context for the second
expression and the effect of the second expression to be
more
urgent
than the priority of the type of the first expression (that is, by then,
reduced to a value). Rule
T-Pair
follows a similar reasoning.

Rule
T-LetPair
has a proviso stating that the effect of
e_{1}

is lower than the lowest priority of the typing environment for
e_{2}
.

T-LetUnit
requires that the channels used for evaluating

e_{1}
have lowest priority than those occurring in
e_{2}
.
This constraint enforces an increasing order of priorities between successive operations, thus complementing the omission of an analogous constraint in rule
F-Seq
, as observed in
Section

3
.

Rule

T-Sel
assigns no effect (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}
) to the evaluation of

\mathsf{select}\,{k}
, requires the priority of the choice operation to be
lower than that of the continuation types and also that the typing
environment has no channel left, i.e., has priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}
. Rule

T-Match
treats
e_{\ell}
as if it were a function (see
T-AbsLin
) and, according to the call-by-value evaluation strategy,
requires the evaluation of
e
to be more urgent than that of

e_{\ell}
.

Rule
T-Eq
states that an
expression that is well typed against a type is well typed against any
of its equivalent types. Type equivalence has been the subject of much
development for context-free session types
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
;
Almeida et al. 2022
;
Costa et al. 2022a
;
Almeida et al. 2020b
)
.
The judgement of type equivalence used in rule
T-Eq
is the
trivial extension of previous proposals by considering only the comparison
of types and type constructors with exactly the same priority.

Finally,
T-New
requires the typing
environment to be unrestricted and type
S
to be well-formed and
not equivalent

to
\mathsf{Skip}
, to ensure that there are some pending operations. We could have
considered
\mathsf{new}
as a constant; this would require
priorities to be instantiated at the type level and
channels to be created with types already
committed
to a priority.

\displaystyle\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\Gamma|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{\mathsf{typeof}({c})}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\bot}}}\quad\textsc{{T-Const}}\qquad\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\Gamma|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma,x:T}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{x}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\bot}}}\quad\textsc{{T-Var}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\Delta,\alpha::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\Gamma|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}\quad\mathsf{fpv}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}})\in\mathsf{dom}({\Theta})}{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Lambda{\alpha}.{e}}:{\forall{\alpha}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}.{T}}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\bot}}}\quad\textsc{{T-TAbs}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\Gamma|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}\quad\mathsf{fpv}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}})\in\mathsf{dom}({\Theta})}{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\mathbb{\Lambda}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}.{e}}:{{\mathchoice{\hbox to8.31pt{\vbox to7.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.69435pt\lower-3.9055pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}{{}}{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{}}{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 -5.1 M -1.5 -4.86 M -5.3 5.1 M -6.2 4.5 M -1.5 -4.86 L -6.2 4.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.6111pt}{-3.47221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e_{1}}:{\mathsf{Unit}}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}}\quad{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{2}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e_{2}}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{2}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho_{1}}<_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\Gamma_{2}|_{\Theta}}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1}\circ\Gamma_{2}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{{e_{1}};{e_{2}}}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}\sqcup{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{2}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{T-LetUnit}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}\quad{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{S_{\ell}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}<_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|S_{\ell}|_{\Theta}}\kern 5.0pt(\forall\ell\in L)\quad k\in L\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\Gamma|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\mathsf{select}\,{k}}:{{\oplus^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}\{{\ell}\colon{S_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in L}}\rightarrow_{m}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}}}{S_{k}}}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\bot}}}\quad\textsc{{T-Sel}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{\&^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}\{{\ell}\colon{S_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in L}}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}<_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\Gamma_{2}|_{\Theta}}\quad{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{2}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e_{\ell}}:{{S_{\ell}}\rightarrow_{\mathsf{lin}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\Gamma_{2}|_{\Theta}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{2}}}{S}}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}}}\quad(\forall\ell\in L)}{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1}\circ\Gamma_{2}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\mathsf{match}\,{e}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{{\ell}\rightarrow{e_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in L}}:{S}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}\sqcup{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{2}}}}\sqcup{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}}}}\quad\textsc{{T-Match}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}\quad{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{S}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\Gamma|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}\quad\Delta\mid\Theta\vdash S\not\simeq\mathsf{Skip}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\mathsf{new}\,{S}}:{{S}\times{\overline{S}}}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\bot}}}\quad\textsc{{T-New}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{T_{1}}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}\quad\Delta\mid\Theta\vdash T_{1}\simeq T_{2}}{{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{T_{2}}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}}\quad\textsc{{T-Eq}}

Figure 11.
Typing rules for expressions.

Fig.

12
presents the typing rules for configurations.
Rules
C-Child
and
C-Main
distinguish the typing of child and main threads:
while any expression (closed for priority and polymorphic variables)
with unrestricted type can run in the main thread, only expressions
with type
\mathsf{Unit}
can compose a child thread. Rules
C-Par

and
C-New
are standard: the former splits the context and
considers the greatest flag for the typing judgement, and the latter introduces two dual
endpoints of a channel in the typing context.

\displaystyle\frac{{\epsilon}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\epsilon}\vdash{e}:{\mathsf{Unit}}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{\circ}{{\circ}\langle{e}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{C-Child}}\qquad\frac{{\epsilon}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\epsilon}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|T|_{\epsilon}}=_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{\bullet}{{\bullet}\langle{e}\rangle}}\quad\textsc{{C-Main}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\Gamma_{1}}\vdash^{\phi_{1}}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\quad{\Gamma_{2}}\vdash^{\phi_{2}}{\mathcal{C}_{2}}}{{\Gamma_{1}\circ\Gamma_{2}}\vdash^{\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}}{{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\,\|\,{\mathcal{C}_{2}}}}\quad\textsc{{C-Par}}\qquad\frac{{\Gamma,x:S,y:\overline{S}}\vdash^{\phi}{\mathcal{C}}\quad{\epsilon}\mid{\epsilon}\vdash{S}}{{\Gamma}\vdash^{\phi}{(\nu{x}{y})\,{\mathcal{C}}}}\quad\textsc{{C-New}}

Combination of thread flags

\displaystyle\circ+\circ=\circ\quad\circ+\;\bullet=\bullet\quad\bullet+\;\circ=\bullet\quad(\text{no rule for }\bullet+\;\bullet)

Figure 12.
Typing rules for configurations.

5.
Dynamics: Type Preservation and Deadlock Freedom

Operational semantics.

Section

5
presents the expression and
configuration reduction rules considering a call-by-value evaluation
strategy. Evaluation
contexts also reflect the left-to-right evaluation strategy and
are defined by the grammar at the top of
Section

5
.
The expression reduction rules are standard for a call-by-value
lambda calculus, except that we have added a new
\beta
-reduction rule for
priority-level abstractions (
E-PApp
).

Configuration reduction includes
rules to lift reductions from expressions to configurations
(
R-LiftE
) and from configurations to configuration contexts
(
R-LiftC
).
R-Fork
creates a new
child
thread,
and
R-New
creates a new channel and binds its endpoints in
a restriction. Rules
R-Com
,
R-Ch
and
R-Close

match, respectively, send and receive, select and match,
close and wait operations in parallel threads. Configuration reduction
also includes the usual rules for parallel execution
(
R-Par
), reduction under restriction (
R-Bind
)
and reduction up to structural congruence (
R-Cong
).

Evaluation contexts

\displaystyle E\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle[{}]\;\mid\;{E}{e}\;\mid\;{v}{E}\;\mid\;\mathsf{let}\,({x_{1}},{x_{2}})={E}\,\mathsf{in}\,{e}\;\mid\;{E};{e}\;\mid\;({E},{e})

(Evaluation contexts)

\displaystyle\;\mid\;({v},{E})\;\mid\;\mathsf{match}\,{E}\,\mathsf{with}\,\{{\ell}\rightarrow{e_{\ell}}\}_{\ell\in L}

(Thread evaluation contexts)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle{\phi}\langle{E}\rangle

(Configuration contexts)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\;::=\;\;

\displaystyle[{}]\;\mid\;{\mathcal{G}}\,\|\,{\mathcal{C}}\;\mid\;(\nu{x}{y})\,{\mathcal{G}}

Expression reduction

\displaystyle\frac{}{{{\lambda_{m}{x}:{\_}.{e}}{v}}\rightarrow{{e}[{v}/{x}]}}\quad\textsc{{E-App}}\quad\frac{}{{\mathsf{let}\,({x_{1}},{x_{2}})={({v_{1}},{v_{2}})}\,\mathsf{in}\,{e}}\rightarrow{{{e}[{v_{1}}/{x_{1}}]}[{v_{2}}/{x_{2}}]}}\quad\textsc{{E-PairElim}}

\displaystyle\frac{}{{{\,()\,};{e}}\rightarrow{e}}\quad\textsc{{E-UnitElim}}\qquad\frac{}{{{(\Lambda{\alpha}.{v})}[{T}]}\rightarrow{{v}[{T}/{\alpha}]}}\quad\textsc{{E-TApp}}\qquad\frac{}{{{(\mathbb{\Lambda}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}.{v})}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}\rightarrow{{v}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}}\quad\textsc{{E-PApp}}

\displaystyle\frac{{e_{1}}\rightarrow{e_{2}}}{{{E}[{e_{1}}]}\rightarrow{{E}[{e_{2}}]}}\quad\textsc{{E-Ctx}}
