Auxiliary Results

We start by proving that structural congruence (
\equiv\,
, cf.
Section

5
, bottom) is type preserving (
Theorem

5.5
).
To this end, we require the following auxiliary results.

Lemma 5.1 (Strengthening).

(1)

If
{\Delta,\alpha::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}
and
\alpha\not\in\mathsf{ftv}({T})
then
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}
.

(2)

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma,x:T}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{U}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}

and
x\not\in\mathsf{fv}({e})
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|T|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}

and
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{U}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}
.

(3)

If
{\Gamma,x:T}\vdash^{\phi}{\mathcal{C}}
and
x\not\in\mathsf{fv}({\mathcal{C}})

then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|T|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}
and
{\Gamma}\vdash^{\phi}{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

By rule induction on the first hypotheses.
∎

Lemma 5.2.

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{S}
then
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\overline{S}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{S}
.
∎

Lemma 5.3 (Agreement for process formation).

(1)

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}

then
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}
and
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}
.

(2)

If
{\Gamma}\vdash^{\phi}{\mathcal{C}}
then
{\epsilon}\mid{\epsilon}\vdash{\Gamma}
.

Proof.

(1)

By induction on the derivation of
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}
.

(2)

By induction on the derivation of
{\Gamma}\vdash^{\phi}{\mathcal{C}}
using item 1. ∎

Lemma 5.4 (Weakening).

(1)

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}
then
{\Delta,\alpha::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}
.

(2)

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}
then
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}}\vdash{T}
.

(3)

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}
then
{\Delta,\alpha::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}
.

(4)

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}
then
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}}\vdash{\Gamma}
.

(5)

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Delta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}
then

{\Delta,\alpha::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Delta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}
.

(6)

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Delta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|U|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}
then

{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma,x:U}\mid{\Delta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}
.

(7)

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Delta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}
then

{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}
.

(8)

If
{\Gamma}\vdash^{\phi}{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|T|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}
then
{\Gamma,x:T}\vdash^{\phi}{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the first hypothesis, for each item.
∎

We then have:

Theorem 5.5.

If
\mathcal{C}\equiv\,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
{\Gamma}\vdash^{\phi}{\mathcal{C}}

then
{\Gamma}\vdash^{\phi}{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

By rule induction on the derivation of
\mathcal{C}\equiv\,\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, using the above lemmas.
∎

5.1.
Type Preservation

We start by
showing that values produce no effect and the priority of the context coincides with priority of their type.

Lemma 5.6.

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{v}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}
then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|\Gamma|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|T|_{\Theta}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{v}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}
.
∎

The next lemma is adapted from Almeida et
al.
(
Almeida et al. 2022
)
and is used to prove
type preservation for communication reductions.

Lemma 5.7.

Let
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}={\Gamma_{1}}\circ{\Gamma_{2}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}\sqcup{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{2}}}
. We have

{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{{E}[{e}]}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}

if and only if

{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{U}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}}

and

{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{2},x:U}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{{E}[{x}]}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{2}}}
.

Proof.

By rule induction on the structure of the evaluation context
E
, in both directions.
∎

The usual substitution lemmas now consider also priority substitution.

Lemma 5.8 (Value substitution).

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}={\Gamma_{1}}\circ{\Gamma_{2}}
and

{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1},x:U}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}}

and
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{2}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{v}:{U}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{2}}}

then
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{{e}[{v}/{x}]}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1},x:U}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}}
,
using
Lemma

5.6
.
∎

Lemma 5.9 (Type substitution).

(1)

Suppose that
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{U}
. If

{\Delta,\alpha::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|U|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}

then

{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{{T}[{U}/{\alpha}]}
.

(2)

If
{\Delta,\alpha::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}={\Gamma_{1}}\circ{\Gamma_{2}}

and
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{U}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|U|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}

then
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{{\Gamma}[{U}/{\alpha}]}={{\Gamma_{1}}[{U}/{\alpha}]}\circ{{\Gamma_{2}}[{U}/{\alpha}]}

(3)

If
{\Delta,\alpha::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}}

and
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{U}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|U|_{\Theta}}=_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}

then
{\Delta}\mid{{\Gamma}[{U}/{\alpha}]}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{{e}[{U}]}:{{T}[{U}/{\alpha}]}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}}
.

Proof.

(1)

By induction on the derivation of
{\Delta,\alpha::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{T}
.

(2)

By induction on the derivation of
{\Delta,\alpha::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{\Gamma}={\Gamma_{1}}\circ{\Gamma_{2}}
.

(3)

By induction on the derivation of
{\Delta,\alpha::{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}}
,
using items 1 and 2.∎

Lemma 5.10 (Priority substitution).

(1)

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}}\vdash{T}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}\in_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}

then
{{\Delta}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}\mid{{\Theta}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}\vdash{{T}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}
.

(2)

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}}\vdash{\Gamma}={\Gamma_{1}}\circ{\Gamma_{2}}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}\in_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}
then

{{\Delta}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}\mid{{\Theta}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}\vdash{{\Gamma}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}={{\Gamma_{1}}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}\circ{{\Gamma_{2}}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}
.

(3)

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}\in_{\Theta}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}

then
{{\Delta}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}\mid{{\Gamma}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}\mid{{\Theta}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}\vdash{{e}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}:{{T}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}/{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}}]}}
.

Proof.

(1)

By induction on the derivation of
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}}\vdash{T}
.

(2)

By induction on the derivation of
{\Delta}\mid{\Theta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}}\vdash{\Gamma}={\Gamma_{1}}\circ{\Gamma_{2}}
.

(3)

By induction on the derivation of
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\iota}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varphi}}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}}

using items 1 and 2.∎

Lemma 5.11 (Expression reduction is type preserving).

If
{e_{1}}\rightarrow{e_{2}}
and
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e_{1}}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}

then
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e_{2}}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
{e_{1}}\rightarrow{e_{2}}
.
∎

Theorem 5.12 (Configuration reduction is type preserving).

If
{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}_{2}}
and
{\Gamma}\vdash^{\phi}{\mathcal{C}_{1}}

then
{\Gamma}\vdash^{\phi}{\mathcal{C}_{2}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the derivation of
{\mathcal{C}_{1}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}_{2}}
; for communication reductions use
Lemma

5.7
.
∎

5.2.
Progress and deadlock-freedom

We now focus on our ultimate goal of proving that well-typed
(closed) configurations are guaranteed to be deadlock-free.

To facilitate the presentation, we
introduce a few concepts, adapted from the work by Kokke and Dardha
(
Kokke and Dardha 2023
)
, that will allow us to describe the state of
communication in a given expression.
We say that an expression
acts on a channel
x
if it is
of the form

•

{{{{{{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}}[{S}]}{v}}{x}
, or
{{{{{\mathsf{receive}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}}[{S}]}{x}
,

•

{{\mathsf{close}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}{x}
, or
{{\mathsf{wait}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}{x}
.

In this case, the expression is said to be an
action
.

Actions need to be extended to a notion that considers the
other forms of
readiness
to communicate.
We thus define an expression
e_{1}
as
ready
if it
is of the form
{E}[{e_{2}}]
,
where
e_{2}
is of the form
\mathsf{new}\,{S}
or

{{{\mathsf{fork}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}}{e_{3}}
or

e_{2}
acts on channel
x
. In the latter case,

e_{1}
is said to be
ready to act on
x
.

We now prove progress for the term language.

Theorem 5.13.

If
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}
and
for all
x:T\in\Gamma
,
T
is a session type, then:
(i)
e
is a value, or
(ii)
{e}\rightarrow{e^{\prime}}
for some
e^{\prime}
, or
(iii)
e
is ready.

Proof.

By rule induction on the hypothesis
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}
.

Cases T-Var, T-Const, T-TAbs, T-PAbs, T-Abs, T-Sel.

Variables, constants, term, type and priority abstractions, as well as
\mathsf{select}\,{k}

are all values.

Case T-App.

In this case,
e={e_{1}}{e_{2}}
,
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\circ\Gamma_{2}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}\sqcup{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{2}}}
.
Premises to the rule include

{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{1}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e_{1}}:{{T_{1}}\rightarrow_{m}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}{T_{2}}}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{1}}}

and

{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{2}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{e_{2}}:{T_{1}}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{2}}}
.
Since
\Gamma
only has session types,
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
also only have sessions.
By induction hypothesis on
e_{1}
, we can have three cases:

Case:
e_{1}
is a value
v_{1}
.
In this case, we proceed by induction on
e_{2}
and
we get three subcases:

•

e_{2}
is a value
v_{2}
. Since
v_{1}
has type
{T_{1}}\rightarrow_{m}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}}{T_{2}}
, either:

v_{1}=\lambda_{m}{x}:{U}.{e}
, in which case
e
reduces by
E-App
, o

v_{1}=\mathsf{select}\,{k}
, which yields
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma_{2}}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{v_{2}}:{\oplus^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}^{\prime}}}\{{\ell}\colon{S_{\ell}}\}_{L}}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}_{2}}}

and so, by inversion on the typing rules,
v_{2}=x
and
e
is ready, or

v_{1}={{{{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}}[{S}]
, in which case

{v_{1}}{v_{2}}
is a value, or

v_{1}={{{{{\mathsf{send}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}}[{S}]}{v}
: similarly to

\mathsf{select}\,{k}
, by inversion we should have
v_{2}=x
and so
e
is ready, or

v_{1}={{{{\mathsf{receive}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}[{T}]}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}}[{S}],{\mathsf{close}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}^{\prime}}\},{\mathsf{wait}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}^{\prime}}\}
, were it is similar, or

v_{1}={{\mathsf{fork}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}^{\prime}}\}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}^{\prime\prime}}\}
, in which case
{v_{1}}{v_{2}}
is ready.

•

e_{2}
reduces. Take
E={v_{1}}{[{}]}
and
e
reduces by
E-Ctx
.

•

e_{2}
is ready and has the form
{E}[{e_{2}^{\prime}}]
. Take
E^{\prime}={v_{1}}{E}

and conclude that
e
is ready.

Case:
e_{1}
reduces.
Take
E={[{}]}{e_{2}}
and
e
reduces by
E-Ctx
.

Case:
e_{1}
is ready and has the form
{E}[{e_{1}^{\prime}}]
.
Take
E^{\prime}={E}{e_{2}}
and
conclude that
e
is ready.

Cases T-TApp, T-PApp
are similar.

Case T-Pair
. In this case,
e=({e_{1}},{e_{2}})
. By induction hypothesis we
know that either both
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are values, in which case
e
is a value,
or one of them is not a value, say
e_{1}
. If
e_{1}
reduces, consider the
context
E=({[{}]},{e_{2}})
and conclude that
e
reduces by
E-Ctx
.
If
e_{1}
is ready and has the form
{E}[{e_{1}^{\prime}}]
,
consider the context
({{E}[{e_{1}^{\prime}}]},{e_{2}})
and conclude that
e
is also ready.

Case T-LetPair
. In this case
e=\mathsf{let}\,({x_{1}},{x_{2}})={e_{1}}\,\mathsf{in}\,{e_{2}}
. By induction
hypothesis on
e_{1}
we know that either:
e_{1}
is a value, in which case
e_{1}=({v_{1}},{v_{2}})

and so
e
reduces by
E-PairElim
; or
e_{1}
reduces, in which case we
consider
E=\mathsf{let}\,({x_{1}},{x_{2}})={[{}]}\,\mathsf{in}\,{e_{2}}
to conclude that
e
reduces;
or
e_{1}
is ready and has the form
{E}[{e_{1}^{\prime}}]
we consider
E^{\prime}=\mathsf{let}\,({x_{1}},{x_{2}})={{E}[{e_{1}^{\prime}}]}\,\mathsf{in}\,{e_{2}}

and conclude that
e
is ready.

Cases T-LetUnit, T-Match
are similar.

Case T-New
. In this case,
e=\mathsf{new}\,{S}
. So,
e
is ready by definition.
∎

Following the work by Kokke and Dardha, we introduce a
notion of canonical forms for configurations. We
say that a configuration
\mathcal{C}
is in
canonical form

if it is of the form
(\nu{x_{1}}{y_{1}})\,{\ldots(\nu{x_{n}}{y_{n}})\,{{\bullet}\langle{e}\rangle}}

or, when it has child threads, it is of the form

(\nu{x_{1}}{y_{1}})\,{\ldots(\nu{x_{n}}{y_{n}})\,{({{\circ}\langle{e_{1}}\rangle}\,\|\,{{\ldots}\,\|\,{{{\circ}\langle{e_{m}}\rangle}\,\|\,{{\bullet}\langle{e}\rangle}}})}},

where no expression
e_{i}
is a value.

Lemma 5.14.

If
{\Gamma}\vdash^{\bullet}{\mathcal{C}}
then there exists some

\mathcal{D}
such that
\mathcal{C}\equiv\,\mathcal{D}
and

\mathcal{D}
is in canonical form.

Proof.

It follows from structural congruence,
observing that the only values that child threads might have
are of the form
\,()\,
.
∎

Intuitively, we say that a configuration is
deadlock-free

if either it reduces forever or, if it terminates, then
all child threads are able to reduce to unit and the main thread
reduces to an unrestricted value. More precisely:

Definition 5.15.

\mathcal{C}
is
deadlock-free
if

\mathcal{C}\rightarrow^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\not\rightarrow
then

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\equiv\,{\bullet}\langle{v}\rangle
, where

v
is such that
{\Delta}\mid{\Gamma}\mid{\Theta}\vdash{v}:{T}\,\&\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}|T|_{\Theta}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}
.

We are finally able to prove deadlock freedom.

Theorem 5.16.

If
{\epsilon}\vdash^{\bullet}{\mathcal{C}}
and
\mathcal{C}
is in
canonical form, then
{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}
for some

\mathcal{D}
or
\mathcal{C}\equiv\,{{\bullet}\langle{v}\rangle}
, where
v
is an unrestricted value.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a configuration in canonical form. Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is of the form:

\mathcal{C}=(\nu{x_{1}}{y_{1}})\,{\ldots(\nu{x_{n}}{y_{n}})\,{({{\circ}\langle{e_{1}}\rangle}\,\|\,{{\ldots}\,\|\,{{{\circ}\langle{e_{m}}\rangle}\,\|\,{{\bullet}\langle{e}\rangle}}})}}.

By
Theorem

5.13
, we know that for each
e_{i}
, either:

•

{e_{i}}\rightarrow{e_{i}^{\prime}}
, in which case we apply
R-LiftM
,
R-LiftC

and find
\mathcal{D}
such that
{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}
, or

•

e_{i}
is ready (provided that it cannot be a value by definition
of canonical form).

Likewise, for
e
, we either have a reduction
{e}\rightarrow{e^{\prime}}

that is lifted to the configuration or, either,
e
is ready or it is a value.
Assume
all expressions

e_{i}
are ready and pick the ready expression with
the smallest priority bound,
e^{\prime}\in\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{m},e\}
.

Case:
e^{\prime}={E}[{\mathsf{new}\,{S}}]
.
We apply
R-New
and
then
R-LiftC
.

Case:
e^{\prime}={E}[{{{{\mathsf{fork}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\rho}}\}}\{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\sigma}}\}}{e_{3}}}]
.
We apply
R-Fork
and then

R-LiftC
.

Case:
e^{\prime}
is an action.
Then, it should be able to act on some
endpoint
x:S
. Let
y:\overline{S}
be the dual endpoint of
x
.
Since channels are linear, there must be an expression
e^{\prime\prime}\in\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{m},e\}\setminus\{e^{\prime}\}

that uses
y
. The expression
e^{\prime\prime}
must either be ready or a value. We analyze these two cases:

Case:
e^{\prime\prime}
is ready.

Expression
e^{\prime\prime}
must be ready to act on
y
, otherwise there would
exist another action on
y
with priority smaller than that of
{e^{\prime\prime}}
,
which contradicts our choice that
e^{\prime}
has lower priority.
Hence,
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
reduce using
E-Com
,
E-CH

or
R-Close
. Then, we apply
R-LiftC
.

Case:
e^{\prime\prime}
is a value.
Since
\mathcal{C}
is in canonical form,

e^{\prime\prime}
can only be a value if
e^{\prime\prime}=e
. Since
e^{\prime\prime}
is a value, it
is either
y
,
({y},{v})
,
({v},{y})
, or
\lambda_{m}{\_}:{\_}.{e^{\prime\prime\prime}}
, where
e^{\prime\prime\prime}

contains
y
. In all these cases typing the expression in the main thread
would not lead to priority
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}
(as is imposed by
C-Main
),
which leads to a contradiction.

Suppose now that
\mathcal{C}=(\nu{x_{1}}{y_{1}})\,{\ldots(\nu{x_{n}}{y_{n}})\,{{\bullet}\langle{e}\rangle}}
. By
Theorem

5.13
we either have a reduction
e\rightarrow e^{\prime}
, or
e
is ready, or
e
is a value. By the reasoning above, if
e
is ready then it cannot be an action, otherwise we would need to have an endpoint in scope with the same (smallest) priority within the same expression, which is not possible; we are then left with
R-New
and
R-Fork
which where analyzed above. If
e
is a value, by the reasoning above it cannot include endpoints and so
v
is an unrestricted value.
∎

Theorem 5.17.

If
{\epsilon}\vdash^{\bullet}{\mathcal{C}}
, then
\mathcal{C}

is deadlock-free.

Proof.

By
Lemma

5.14
, there exists
\mathcal{D}_{1}

in canonical form
such that
\mathcal{C}\equiv\,\mathcal{D}_{1}
.
We have that either
\mathcal{D}_{1}
reduces forever, in which case
\mathcal{C}
is deadlock-free, or
we apply
Theorem

5.16
, followed
by
Theorems

5.12
and
5.14

to get
\mathcal{D}_{1}\rightarrow^{*}{\mathcal{D}_{n}}
, where

{\epsilon}\vdash^{\bullet}{\mathcal{D}_{n}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{n}\not\rightarrow
.
To show that
\mathcal{D}_{n}\equiv\,{\bullet}\langle{v}\rangle
, we
first prove that
\mathcal{D}_{n}
does not contain any session type in scope.

For that purpose, let us assume that

\mathcal{D}_{n}=(\nu{x}{y})\,{\mathcal{D}_{n+1}}
.
By
C-New
we get
{x:S,y:\overline{S}}\vdash^{\bullet}{\mathcal{D}_{n+1}}
.
That is,
x
and
y
occur in
\mathcal{D}_{n+1}
and have
compatible types. Thus, one of the following reductions should hold on

\mathcal{D}_{n}
:

R-Com
,
R-Ch
or
R-Close
. This contradicts our
hypothesis that
\mathcal{D}_{n}\not\rightarrow
, so this case does not hold.

This means that
\mathcal{D}_{n}
does not have any restrictions. Since it is
well-typed under the
\epsilon
context,
\mathcal{D}_{n}

should be closed. Then, it must be structurally congruent to

{\bullet}\langle{v}\rangle
, where
v
is an unrestricted value.
We conclude that
\mathcal{C}
is deadlock-free.
∎

Priorities in action

We now revisit the example from
Section

2.4

to show that it is well-formed.
The complete program should include
a
main
function, as well as functions
server
and
client
, which send and receive integer values:

⬇

from

The typing derivation starts by applying

and

twice to put

,

in the context. Then, it isolates the

using

.
To check the typing of the first

, we apply

to split

from the typing of the

\

\rightarrow

{1}

.
The typing of

succeeds after applying

twice, to abstract over the priorities—while checking that
the priority instantiation

belongs to the

({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}}]
and

belongs to
[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bot}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\top}}]
, as prescribed in the

(

).
For

, followed by

and are left with

and

against the types provided

To check the rest of the program, we
apply

{1}{2}
.
We apply

to the arguments

and

. Although we have not detailed typing rules for integers, booleans
and conditionals (for the sake of simplicity), we can easily adapt

(

)
) to check the

\

\rightarrow

).
Finally, we use

twice to check that

{1}{2}
conforms to the provided type.

We would like to point out a small detail about notation. According to our proposal, the primitive
new
is invoked over a session type (cf. Rule
T-New
in
Fig.

11
). In contrast, in the current syntax of FreeST,
new
expects a type application followed by application to unit (see the
main
function presented in
Section

2.1
). This minor discrepancy is due to our choice of not having
new
as a constant, as discussed in
Section

4
, and is in line with former proposals of term languages for context-free session types
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
;
Almeida et al. 2022
)
.

6.
Related Work

Closely related work has been discussed throughout the paper; here we provide a broader context by commenting on other works.

Session Types

Session types were proposed by Honda et al.
(
Honda et al. 1998
;
Honda 1993
)

as a way of structuring communication in heterogeneously typed channels. The
original theory of session types has been extended in many ways, including
incorporating bounded polymorphism
(
Gay 2008
;
Dardha et al. 2017
)
, label
dependency
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2019
)
and types with non-regular recursion via
context-free session types
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
)
.

Our work
capitalizes on developments for context-free session types,
including extensions with higher-order types
(
Costa et al. 2022b
)
, impredicative polymorphism
(
Almeida et al. 2022
)
,
higher-order polymorphism
(
Poças et al. 2023
)
, algorithms for type equivalence
(
Almeida et al. 2020a
)
, subtyping
(
Silva et al. 2023
)
and kind inference
(
Almeida et al. 2023
;
Almeida et al. 2025
)
. These
developments have been integrated in the compiler of the FreeST programming language
(
Almeida et al. 2019
)
.
This line of work has focused on increasing the expressiveness of types, adopting the view that programmers should have the liberty of writing typable programs with deadlocked behaviors.
Our work shows that aiming at expressivity does not really conflict with strong, type-based correctness guarantees, as context-free session types can be effectively extended with deadlock-freedom guarantees while still supporting programs not expressible in standard session types.

Type Systems for Deadlock Freedom

The priority-based approach that we have incorporated on context-free session types can be traced back to seminal work by Kobayashi
(
Kobayashi 2002
;
Kobayashi 2006
)
, who developed several type systems based on priorities for statically enforcing (dead)lock freedom for
\pi
-calculus processes, under linear type systems with
usages
. The resulting classes of concurrent processes are deadlock-free by typing, and rather expressive: they can express process networks in cyclic topologies and encode typed
\lambda
-calculi and the sequencing behavior distinctive of session types
(
Kobayashi 2002
;
Dardha et al. 2017
)
.

Caires and Pfenning
(
Caires and Pfenning 2010
)
established
a Curry-Howard correspondence between session types and intuitionistic linear logic, from which correctness guarantees such as
deadlock freedom and confluence follow from proof-theoretical principles.
Wadler
(
Wadler 2012
)
transferred this connection to the classical setting.
This Curry-Howard approach to session types enabled a number of extensions, including forms of
typed observational equivalence
(
Pérez et al. 2014
)

and dependent types
(
Toninho et al. 2011
)
as
well as integrations of processes, functions, and sessions
(
Toninho et al. 2013
)
, to name just a few.
Also in this line we find nested session types
(
Das et al. 2021
)
, which were proved to strictly extend context-free session types.
An outstanding limitation of the logic-based approach to session types is its support for tree-like topologies only, which leaves out many real-world scenarios
(
Dardha and Pérez 2022
)
.
Extensions of the logic-based approach with priorities, in order to support cyclic topologies, have been proposed in

(
Dardha and Gay 2018
)
(for synchronous processes without recursive types) and in
(
van den Heuvel and Pérez 2021
;
van den Heuvel and Pérez 2024
)
(for asynchronous processes with recursive types).

From Processes to Programs

Strictly speaking, all previously mentioned works concern processes, not programs.
Although processes are long known to encode concurrent programs, the difference is relevant, as the non-local analyses enabled by priorities can be more directly performed for processes (where communication constructs are explicit) than for programs (where communication arises via functional constructs, cf.
Fig.

10
).
To our knowledge, Padovani and Novara were the first to adapt a type system for deadlock-freedom for processes
(
Padovani 2014a
)
to the case of higher-order concurrent programs
(
Padovani and Novara 2015
)
(see also their technical report
(
Padovani and Novara 2014
)
); a source of inspiration for our developments, their work uses recursion and polymorphism, although in a limited way (see the discussion in
(
Padovani and Novara 2014
)
).
The example in
Section

2.4
illustrates the expressiveness of our approach with respect to Padovani and Novara’s.
Our work has been also influenced by the work of Kokke and Dardha
(
Kokke and Dardha 2023
)
, who adapted Padovani and Novara’s approach to the case of regular session types without recursion, which is subsumed by our work.

7.
Conclusion

In this work, we have extended context-free session types
for higher-order concurrent programs
(
Thiemann and Vasconcelos 2016
;
Almeida et al. 2020a
)

with
priority-based mechanisms that
deadlock freedom
(
Padovani and Novara 2014
;
Kokke and Dardha 2023
;
Padovani and Novara 2015
)
.
Our type system supports polymorphism and non-regular recursion,
while providing strong correctness guarantees for communicating programs.

We find it remarkable that techniques for excluding deadlocks for linear types and regular session types can also be applied to the more expressive context-free session types. Intuitively, this can be interpreted as follows: deadlocks are a purely operational matter, tied to the underlying message-passing model, rather than to the (rich) type structures that govern communication behavior. Still, it is reassuring to confirm that priority-based approaches scale to the expressive and developed setting of context-free sessions.

Although foundational, our developments have a concrete practical dimension.
The design of our framework is aligned with FreeST, a programming
language with context-free session types.
Ongoing work concerns the incorporation of the type system presented here in FreeST’s compiler.
Currently, the implementation uses an SMT solver (Z3
(
De Moura and Bjørner 2008
)
) to decide the
satisfiability of the priority restrictions collected during type checking. Finalizing and evaluating the implementation is part of future work.
